 Part 2 — Bakry–Émery Γ(_2) Calculus and the Curvature–Dimension Condition (CD(\rho,\infty))

*(This is written so it can be dropped in as Part 2/20 of the full document.)*

---

## 2.1. Setting

Let ((M,g)) be a smooth, connected, complete, finite-dimensional Riemannian manifold.
Let (S\in C^2(M)) be a potential such that the Gibbs normalization constant
[
Z := \int_M e^{-S(x)},d\mathrm{vol}_g(x)
]
is finite. We define the Gibbs measure
[
d\mu(x) := Z^{-1} e^{-S(x)},d\mathrm{vol}_g(x).
]

We work throughout with the **Riemannian volume as reference measure**; this is the convention used later for lattice Yang–Mills, where (d\mathrm{vol}_g) will be the product Haar volume on the configuration manifold.

The associated diffusion generator is
[
L f = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S,\nabla f\rangle,
]
initially defined on (C_c^\infty(M)) and then viewed as the generator of a (\mu)-symmetric Markov semigroup ((P_t)_{t\ge0}) on (L^2(\mu)). Here (\Delta_g) is the Laplace–Beltrami operator and (\nabla) the Riemannian gradient.

The associated **carré du champ** (square field) is
[
\Gamma(f,g) := \langle \nabla f,\nabla g\rangle,
\qquad
\Gamma(f) := \Gamma(f,f) = |\nabla f|^2.
]

The Dirichlet form associated with (L) is
[
\mathcal{E}(f,g) := \int_M \Gamma(f,g),d\mu
= \int_M \langle \nabla f,\nabla g\rangle,d\mu.
]
As recalled in Section 1, ((\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}))) is a closed, Markovian form and there exists a unique nonnegative self-adjoint operator (-L) with
[
\mathcal{E}(f,g) = \langle f,-Lg\rangle_{L^2(\mu)}
\quad
\forall f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}),\ \forall g\in\mathcal{D}(-L).
]

---

## 2.2. Basic Γ-calculus identities

The carré du champ inherits several formal properties from the diffusion generator (L). For (f,g,h\in C^\infty(M)), we have:

1. **Symmetry:**
   [
   \Gamma(f,g) = \Gamma(g,f).
   ]

2. **Leibniz rule:**
   [
   \Gamma(fg,h) = f,\Gamma(g,h) + g,\Gamma(f,h).
   ]

3. **Chain rule:**
   For (\Phi\in C^1(\mathbb{R})),
   [
   \Gamma(\Phi(f),g) = \Phi'(f),\Gamma(f,g).
   ]

These are straightforward consequences of (\Gamma(f,g) = \frac12\big(L(fg)-fLg-gLf\big)) together with the diffusion property of (L) (i.e. (L) is a second-order differential operator without zeroth-order term). They will be used tacitly in the sequel.

---

## 2.3. The iterated carré du champ Γ(_2)

The **iterated carré du champ** (Bakry–Émery Γ(_2) operator) is defined by
[
\Gamma_2(f,g)
:= \frac12\big(L\Gamma(f,g) - \Gamma(f,Lg) - \Gamma(g,Lf)\big),
\qquad
\Gamma_2(f) := \Gamma_2(f,f).
]

In our Riemannian setting with (L = \Delta_g - \langle\nabla S,\nabla\cdot\rangle) and (\Gamma(f,g)=\langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle), (\Gamma_2(f)) can be expressed geometrically in terms of the Hessian of (f), the Ricci curvature of ((M,g)), and the Hessian of the potential (S). Deriving this identity is the content of the Bochner–Bakry–Émery calculation below.

---

## 2.4. Bochner identity for the Laplace–Beltrami operator

We first recall the classical Bochner identity for the Laplace–Beltrami operator (\Delta_g) on ((M,g)). Let (\nabla^2 f) denote the Riemannian Hessian of (f), and define the **Hilbert–Schmidt norm** of (\nabla^2 f) at a point (x\in M) by
[
|\nabla^2 f(x)|*{\mathrm{HS}}^2
= \sum*{i,j=1}^n \big(\nabla^2 f(e_i,e_j)\big)^2,
]
for any orthonormal basis ({e_i}_{i=1}^n) of (T_xM).

The **Bochner identity** states that for all (f\in C^\infty(M)),
[
\frac12 \Delta_g |\nabla f|^2
= |\nabla^2 f|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2

* \langle \nabla f,\nabla(\Delta_g f)\rangle
* \operatorname{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f),
  ]
  where (\operatorname{Ric}_g) is the Riemannian Ricci tensor. Equivalently,
  [
  \frac12\Big(
  \Delta_g|\nabla f|^2

- 2\langle\nabla f,\nabla(\Delta_g f)\rangle
  \Big)
  = |\nabla^2 f|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 + \operatorname{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f).
  ]

This identity is purely geometric: it depends only on ((M,g)), not on the potential (S).

---

## 2.5. Full Bochner–Bakry–Émery computation

We now compute (\Gamma_2(f)) for the generator
[
L f = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S,\nabla f\rangle,
]
using the definition
[
\Gamma_2(f)
= \frac12\big(L|\nabla f|^2 - 2\langle \nabla f,\nabla(Lf)\rangle\big).
]

### 2.5.1. Decomposition of (\Gamma_2(f))

Compute the two pieces separately:

1. **First term: (L|\nabla f|^2).** Using (L = \Delta_g - \langle\nabla S,\nabla\cdot\rangle),
   [
   L|\nabla f|^2
   = \Delta_g |\nabla f|^2

   * \langle\nabla S,\nabla|\nabla f|^2\rangle.
     ]

2. **Second term: (\nabla(Lf)).** Since
   [
   Lf = \Delta_g f - \langle\nabla S,\nabla f\rangle,
   ]
   we have
   [
   \nabla(Lf)
   = \nabla(\Delta_g f) - \nabla\langle\nabla S,\nabla f\rangle.
   ]

Substituting into the definition of (\Gamma_2(f)) gives
[
\begin{aligned}
\Gamma_2(f)
&= \frac12\Big[
\Delta_g|\nabla f|^2
- \langle\nabla S,\nabla|\nabla f|^2\rangle \
&\qquad\qquad
- 2\langle\nabla f,\nabla(\Delta_g f)\rangle
+ 2\langle\nabla f,\nabla\langle\nabla S,\nabla f\rangle\rangle
\Big].
\end{aligned}
]

Now apply the Bochner identity for (\Delta_g):
[
\frac12\Delta_g|\nabla f|^2
= |\nabla^2 f|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2

* \langle \nabla f,\nabla(\Delta_g f)\rangle
* \operatorname{Ric}*g(\nabla f,\nabla f).
  ]
  Substituting this into the expression for (\Gamma_2(f)) yields
  [
  \begin{aligned}
  \Gamma_2(f)
  &= \big(|\nabla^2 f|*{\mathrm{HS}}^2

  * \langle\nabla f,\nabla(\Delta_g f)\rangle
  * \operatorname{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f)\big) \
    &\quad - \frac12\langle\nabla S,\nabla|\nabla f|^2\rangle

  - \langle\nabla f,\nabla(\Delta_g f)\rangle

  * \langle\nabla f,\nabla\langle\nabla S,\nabla f\rangle\rangle.
    \end{aligned}
    ]

The (\langle\nabla f,\nabla(\Delta_g f)\rangle) terms cancel, leaving
[
\Gamma_2(f)
= |\nabla^2 f|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2

* \operatorname{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f)

- \tfrac12\langle\nabla S,\nabla|\nabla f|^2\rangle

* \langle\nabla f,\nabla\langle\nabla S,\nabla f\rangle\rangle.
  ]

### 2.5.2. Simplifying the (S)-dependent terms

We now show that the last two terms combine into the Hessian of (S) evaluated on (\nabla f). Write (\nabla^2 S) for the Riemannian Hessian of (S). For vector fields (X,Y),
[
\nabla^2 S(X,Y)
= X\langle\nabla S,Y\rangle - \langle\nabla S,\nabla_X Y\rangle.
]

A standard computation in normal coordinates (or any standard reference on Riemannian geometry) shows that
[
-\tfrac12\langle\nabla S,\nabla|\nabla f|^2\rangle

* \langle\nabla f,\nabla\langle\nabla S,\nabla f\rangle\rangle
  = \nabla^2 S(\nabla f,\nabla f).
  ]

Sketch: work in a local orthonormal frame ({e_i}) with (\nabla_{e_i}e_j=0) at the point (x). Write (\nabla f = \sum_i f_i e_i), (f_i = e_i f), and (S_i = e_i S). One checks that
[
|\nabla f|^2 = \sum_i f_i^2,
\qquad
\nabla|\nabla f|^2 = 2\sum_i f_i \nabla f_i,
]
and derives explicit formulas for (\langle\nabla S,\nabla|\nabla f|^2\rangle) and (\langle\nabla f,\nabla\langle\nabla S,\nabla f\rangle\rangle). The mixed terms cancel and the remainder is exactly (\nabla^2 S(\nabla f,\nabla f)).

Substituting this identity into the expression for (\Gamma_2(f)) gives the **Bochner–Bakry–Émery identity**:
[
\boxed{
\Gamma_2(f)
= |\nabla^2 f|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2

* \operatorname{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f)
* \nabla^2 S(\nabla f,\nabla f).
  }

]

---

## 2.6. The Bakry–Émery curvature tensor and (CD(\rho,\infty))

The calculation above naturally leads to the **Bakry–Émery Ricci tensor** associated with the Gibbs measure (\mu):

> **Definition 2.1 (Bakry–Émery curvature).**
> For the Gibbs measure (d\mu = Z^{-1}e^{-S}d\mathrm{vol}*g) on ((M,g)), the **Bakry–Émery Ricci tensor** is the symmetric 2-tensor
> [
> \operatorname{Ric}*\mu := \operatorname{Ric}_g + \nabla^2 S.
> ]

In this notation, the Bochner–Bakry–Émery identity can be rewritten as
[
\boxed{
\Gamma_2(f)
= |\nabla^2 f|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2

* \operatorname{Ric}_\mu(\nabla f,\nabla f).
  }
  ]

Since (|\nabla^2 f|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\ge0) pointwise, this immediately suggests the **curvature–dimension condition**.

> **Definition 2.2 (Curvature–dimension condition (CD(\rho,\infty))).**
> We say that ((M,g,\mu)) satisfies (CD(\rho,\infty)) for some (\rho\in\mathbb{R}) if
> [
> \Gamma_2(f)(x) ;\ge; \rho,\Gamma(f)(x)
> \quad\text{for all } f\in C^\infty(M),\ x\in M.
> ]

Using the Bochner–Bakry–Émery identity, this condition is equivalent to a pointwise lower bound on (\operatorname{Ric}_\mu):

> **Proposition 2.3 (Equivalence of (CD(\rho,\infty)) and tensor lower bound).**
> The following are equivalent:
>
> 1. (CD(\rho,\infty)) holds, i.e.
>    [
>    \Gamma_2(f) \ge \rho,\Gamma(f)
>    \quad\text{for all smooth } f.
>    ]
> 2. The Bakry–Émery tensor satisfies
>    [
>    \operatorname{Ric}_\mu(x)(v,v) \ge \rho,|v|_g^2
>    \quad\forall x\in M,\ \forall v\in T_xM.
>    ]

*Proof.*

* (2) (\Rightarrow) (1) is immediate: if (\operatorname{Ric}*\mu\ge \rho g), then for any (f),
  [
  \Gamma_2(f)
  = |\nabla^2 f|*{\mathrm{HS}}^2 + \operatorname{Ric}_\mu(\nabla f,\nabla f)
  \ge 0 + \rho |\nabla f|^2
  = \rho,\Gamma(f).
  ]

* (1) (\Rightarrow) (2): fix a point (x\in M) and a vector (v\in T_xM). We choose a smooth function (f) with
  [
  \nabla f(x) = v,\qquad \nabla^2 f(x) = 0.
  ]
  Such (f) exists by standard local coordinate constructions. Evaluating the Bochner–Bakry–Émery identity at (x), we get
  [
  \Gamma_2(f)(x)
  = |\nabla^2 f(x)|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2

  * \operatorname{Ric}*\mu(x)(v,v)
    = \operatorname{Ric}*\mu(x)(v,v).
    ]
    On the other hand, (CD(\rho,\infty)) implies (\Gamma_2(f)(x)\ge \rho,\Gamma(f)(x) = \rho |v|*g^2). Hence
    [
    \operatorname{Ric}*\mu(x)(v,v) \ge \rho |v|_g^2.
    ]
    Since (x) and (v) were arbitrary, (2) holds. ∎

Thus in finite dimensions the curvature–dimension condition (CD(\rho,\infty)) is equivalent to a genuine geometric lower bound on the Bakry–Émery curvature tensor (\operatorname{Ric}_\mu). This is the sense in which the Gibbs measure (d\mu) is **curvature-controlled**.

This equivalence is used explicitly later when we restate local curvature assumptions in terms of pointwise bounds on (\operatorname{Ric}_\mu) on a region (\Omega\subset M).

---

## 2.7. Stability and examples

### 2.7.1. Strongly convex potentials on (\mathbb{R}^n)

On (\mathbb{R}^n) with the Euclidean metric, (\operatorname{Ric}*g\equiv 0). If (S\colon\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}) is uniformly convex in the sense that
[
\nabla^2 S(x) \ge \rho I_n
\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^n
]
for some (\rho>0), then
[
\operatorname{Ric}*\mu(x) = 0 + \nabla^2 S(x) \ge \rho I_n,
]
so (CD(\rho,\infty)) holds. The associated Gibbs measure (\mu) (e.g. a Gaussian or strongly log-concave measure) therefore satisfies all the consequences of (CD(\rho,\infty)), in particular a Poincaré and a log-Sobolev inequality with constants controlled by (1/\rho) and (2/\rho) respectively (proved in Part I, Section 3).

### 2.7.2. Products

Let ((M_i,g_i,S_i)), (i=1,\dots,N), be finite-dimensional Riemannian manifolds with potentials (S_i\in C^2(M_i)) and Gibbs measures
[
d\mu_i = Z_i^{-1} e^{-S_i} d\mathrm{vol}*{g_i}.
]
Form the product manifold
[
(M,g) := \Big(\prod*{i=1}^N M_i,\ \bigoplus_{i=1}^N g_i\Big),
]
with product metric, and the product potential
[
S(x_1,\dots,x_N) := \sum_{i=1}^N S_i(x_i),
]
so that
[
d\mu = \bigotimes_{i=1}^N \mu_i.
]

In this setting:

* The Ricci tensor of the product is the orthogonal sum
  [
  \operatorname{Ric}*g = \bigoplus*{i=1}^N \operatorname{Ric}_{g_i},
  ]
* The Hessian of (S) is block-diagonal,
  [
  \nabla^2 S = \bigoplus_{i=1}^N \nabla^2 S_i.
  ]

Thus
[
\operatorname{Ric}*\mu = \bigoplus*{i=1}^N \operatorname{Ric}*{\mu_i},
\quad\text{where}\quad \operatorname{Ric}*{\mu_i} = \operatorname{Ric}_{g_i} + \nabla^2 S_i.
]

If each component satisfies
[
\operatorname{Ric}*{\mu_i} \ge \rho,g_i
]
for some common (\rho>0), then the product satisfies
[
\operatorname{Ric}*\mu \ge \rho,g,
]
and hence (CD(\rho,\infty)) holds on the product. This **product stability** of curvature is a crucial ingredient when we pass from a single gauge group (G) to the full lattice configuration manifold (M_\Lambda = G^{E(\Lambda)}) equipped with the product bi-invariant metric and product Haar volume.

---

## 2.8. Specialization to lattice Yang–Mills (formal corollary)

We briefly record the specialization of the Bakry–Émery tensor to the lattice Yang–Mills configuration space; the full details are developed in Part II, but the formula is already used here as a guiding example.

For a finite lattice (\Lambda), let
[
M_\Lambda := G^{E(\Lambda)}
]
with product bi-invariant metric (g_\Lambda). The reference measure is the product Haar volume (d\mathrm{vol}*{g*\Lambda}), and the effective action
[
S_\Lambda(U) = S_W(U) + S_{\mathrm{add},\Lambda}(U)
]
includes the Wilson plaquette action (S_W) and possible additional smooth, gauge-invariant contributions (S_{\mathrm{add},\Lambda}).

The finite-volume YM Gibbs measure is
[
d\mu_\Lambda(U)
:= Z_\Lambda^{-1} e^{-S_\Lambda(U)},d\mathrm{vol}*{g*\Lambda}(U),
]
and the corresponding generator is
[
L_\Lambda f = \Delta_{g_\Lambda} f - \langle\nabla S_\Lambda,\nabla f\rangle_{g_\Lambda}.
]

By the same computation as above, the associated Bakry–Émery tensor is
[
\operatorname{Ric}*{\mu*\Lambda}
= \operatorname{Ric}*{g*\Lambda} + \nabla^2 S_\Lambda.
]

Key geometric facts established in Part II are:

* The configuration manifold has **uniform positive Ricci curvature**:
  [
  \operatorname{Ric}*{g*\Lambda} \ge \kappa_G,g_\Lambda,
  ]
  where (\kappa_G>0) depends only on the Lie group (G) and the normalization of its bi-invariant metric, not on (\Lambda). This is the “Haar mass” contribution.

* The additional potential (S_{\mathrm{add},\Lambda}) has Hessian bounded below by (-C_{\mathrm{add}} g_\Lambda). At the trivial configuration (U^{(0)}), one has on horizontal (physical) directions
  [
  \nabla^2 S_\Lambda(U^{(0)})(v,v)
  \ge -C_{\mathrm{add}},|v|*{g*\Lambda}^2.
  ]

Consequently, at (U^{(0)}) and on horizontal directions (v\in H_{U^{(0)}}),
[
\operatorname{Ric}*{\mu*\Lambda}(v,v)
= \operatorname{Ric}*{g*\Lambda}(v,v)

* \nabla^2 S_\Lambda(U^{(0)})(v,v)
  \ge (\kappa_G - C_{\mathrm{add}}),|v|*{g*\Lambda}^2.
  ]

If (\kappa_G>C_{\mathrm{add}}), this provides a **strictly positive horizontal Bakry–Émery curvature lower bound** at the vacuum configuration. In Part II, Section 6, this pointwise estimate is extended to a small-field neighborhood of (U^{(0)}), yielding a local horizontal curvature–dimension condition that serves as the main YM input into the abstract functional-inequality machinery of Parts III–V.

---

## 2.9. Interface with the rest of the program

The material of this part plays three distinct roles in the overall YM mass-gap program:

1. **Abstract curvature–FI link (Part I, Section 3).**
   The Bochner–Bakry–Émery identity and the (CD(\rho,\infty)) condition are the starting point for proving volume-uniform Poincaré and log-Sobolev inequalities under curvature lower bounds. These results are developed in full in Part I, Section 3.

2. **Abstract interface to gauge theories (Part I, Section 5).**
   The Bakry–Émery tensor (\operatorname{Ric}*\mu) is the geometric object that enters the abstract **gauge-invariant curvature hypothesis** (H*{\mathrm{curv}}) in the interface framework for Yang–Mills. There, one restricts (\operatorname{Ric}_\mu) to horizontal (physical) directions and demands a uniform lower bound on that restriction.

3. **Concrete curvature bounds for lattice YM (Parts II–III).**
   On the concrete lattice configuration manifold (M_\Lambda = G^{E(\Lambda)}), the decomposition
   [
   \operatorname{Ric}*{\mu*\Lambda} = \operatorname{Ric}*{g*\Lambda} + \nabla^2 S_\Lambda
   ]
   allows one to combine:

   * positive Ricci curvature coming from Haar geometry, and
   * controlled Hessians of the Wilson plus regulator action,
     to obtain the **core curvature theorem** of Part II, Section 7, and the corresponding **local curvature–dimension condition** that feeds into the local-to-global FI results of Part III.

These structures are then transported, via the OS reconstruction and comparison between configuration diffusion and Hamiltonian dynamics, into statements about finite-volume spectral gaps and candidate continuum mass gaps, as discussed in Parts IV–V.

---

Part 10. Haar Mass, Effective Potential, and Local Horizontal Bakry–Émery Bound

Role: Put together the Haar curvature and the action Hessian into a local BE curvature lower bound on physical directions.

Content to merge:
	•	PART_II_SECTION_6_Local_Horizontal_Bakry_Emery_Bound.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_6_Local_Horizontal_Bakry_Emery_Bound(1).md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_6_Local_Horizontal_Bakry_Emery_Bound_UPDATED.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_6_Local_Horizontal_Bakry_Emery_Bound_UPDATED(1).md
(Consolidate to the cleanest UPDATED version, supplement with any missing lemmas.)  

Main results:
	•	Expression \mathrm{Ric}_{\mu_\Lambda} = \mathrm{Ric}_{g_\Lambda} + \nabla^2 S_\Lambda and its restriction to H_U.
	•	At U^{(0)}, horizontal curvature bound \mathrm{Ric}_{\mu_\Lambda}(U^{(0)})(v,v)\ge (\kappa_G - C_{\mathrm{add}})|v|^2 with \kappa_G>C_{\mathrm{add}}.
	•	By continuity, existence of a small-field radius r>0 and \rho_{\text{loc}}>0 so that this holds on a ball B_r(U^{(0)}), uniformly in \Lambda.  

This is the YM analogue of the scalar convexity bound: a local CD(ρ,∞) in physical directions near the trivial configuration.

⸻

Part 11. Core Curvature Theorem and Interface Back to Part I

Role: Package Part 6–10 into a single, clean theorem that exactly matches the abstract hypothesis from Part 5.

Content to merge:
	•	PART_II_SECTION_7_Core_Curvature_Theorem_and_Interface_to_Part_I.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_7_Core_Curvature_Theorem_and_Interface_to_Part_I_UPDATED.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_7_Core_Curvature_Theorem_and_Interface_to_Part_I_UPDATED(1).md
	•	The “Technical Addendum” on horizontal BE curvature at the end.

Main results:
	•	Theorem: For effective action S_\Lambda = S_W + S_{\text{add},\Lambda} with local, gauge-invariant and Hessian-bounded S_{\text{add},\Lambda}, plus \mathrm{Ric}_G\ge\kappa_G g_G and \kappa_G>C_{\text{add}}, there exist \rho_{\text{loc}}>0,r>0 independent of \Lambda such that
	•	Horizontal BE curvature \ge \rho_{\text{loc}} on B_r(U^{(0)}),
	•	Hence local CD(\rho_{\text{loc}},\infty) for gauge-invariant observables.  
	•	Explicit restatement as hypothesis H_{\text{curv,loc}} for Part III.

This part is the “this is exactly the input the abstract machinery wants” chapter.

	•	Abstract setting: local CD(ρ_loc,∞) on Ω, Lyapunov W with LW\le -\alpha W + \beta 1_K, local Poincaré on K.  
	•	Theorem: global Poincaré inequality with explicit constant C_P depending only on \rho_{\text{loc}},\alpha,\beta,\mu(K).
	•	Interpretation in finite-volume context: this gives a volume-uniform spectral gap for the diffusion generator once you have a volume-uniform Lyapunov bound.

	•	Specific technical conjectures about curvature, Lyapunov functions, and OS/continuum behavior that would close the gap.
	•	Conceptual “moral”: curvature-controlled Gibbs measures give a plausible analytic path to a mass gap, but certain hard YM-specific estimates remain open.

4.4.3. Relation to physical mass

In a Hamiltonian framework, a physical mass gap typically refers to a lower bound on the spectrum of a quantum Hamiltonian 
H

L

a uniform lower bound on curvature,

implies a uniform spectral gap of a natural generator,

which controls correlation lengths and relaxation times.

The scalar model thus realizes, in a transparent way, the slogan

“Positive curvature (uniform convexity) ⇒ volume-independent spectral gap.”

This geometric backbone is what makes the later curvature estimates for lattice Yang–Mills plausible: the group itself already wants to push you back toward the identity, and the Wilson action and gauge geometry modulate this in a controlled way.

Part 7 — Wilson Action, Gauge Symmetry, and Reflection Positivity (Configuration Level)

In this part we finally put a Yang–Mills interaction on the configuration manifold from Part 6 and record the two structural facts that make the rest of the program possible:

The Wilson plaquette action is a smooth, local, gauge-invariant potential on

(M

At the space–time lattice level, the Wilson weight is reflection positive with respect to Euclidean time reflection, in the classical Osterwalder–Seiler sense.

The first point lets us feed lattice Yang–Mills into the Bakry–Émery curvature machinery. The second is the key OS input for constructing a transfer matrix / Hamiltonian in Part IV.

 

We start in purely spatial dimension for the curvature story, and then lift to a space–time lattice to discuss time reflection and reflection positivity.

The moral is: locality + gauge invariance + the specific plaquette structure of the Wilson action guarantee that the Boltzmann weight can be written as an inner product of a positive-time functional with its reflection, and that is exactly reflection positivity.

In this project, we treat this result as a known structural theorem and do not re-prove each combinatorial step in the main line of the text; the reflection-positivity section contains the detailed lattice-level derivation. 

7.8. Role of reflection positivity in the overall program

At the level of this part:

The spatial Wilson Gibbs measure 
μΛW
μ
Λ
W
	​

 is a perfectly good finite-dimensional Gibbs system in the Bakry–Émery sense and will be the starting point for curvature analysis once we add Haar mass and gauge-fixing contributions in later sections.

The spacetime extension of the Wilson action satisfies reflection positivity with respect to Euclidean time reflection, which is the minimal OS input needed to construct a transfer matrix and a positive self-adjoint Hamiltonian 
HΛ
H
Λ
	​

 in Part 17.

Conceptually:

Curvature side (Parts 6, 9–11):
We modify the Wilson potential by adding Haar-Jacobian and possibly mild “additive” terms, and then compute horizontal Hessians and Ricci curvature in the spatial (configuration) manifold, leading to local Bakry–Émery bounds.

OS / Hamiltonian side (Parts 16–18):
We use reflection positivity and Euclidean invariance of the spacetime Wilson measure to reconstruct a Hilbert space and Hamiltonian. The reflection positivity of the Wilson action is the seed for that.

In the next part (Part 8), we turn back to the configuration manifold and analyze the gauge orbits and the decomposition of tangent space into vertical (pure gauge) and horizontal (physical) directions. That is where we make precise why gradients of gauge-invariant observables live in horizontal subspaces and why only horizontal curvature matters for the physical sector.

The characterization of the kernel as \(\ker d_1\) follows because \[ \|d_1 X\|_{\mathcal{C}^2}^2 = 0 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d_1 X = 0.

In the next parts of the project, this core curvature theorem will be combined with Lyapunov estimates, OS reconstruction, and continuum-limit analysis to formulate precise scenarios under which such configuration-space spectral gaps would imply a genuine mass gap for continuum Yang–Mills.

14.7. Summary and open analytic tasks

Structurally, after Parts I–III and this chapter, the situation can be summarized as follows. 

Geometry (Part II):
Haar geometry + Wilson action + gauge structure give a local horizontal Bakry–Émery curvature bound on a small-field region

, with curvature constant

Analysis (Part III §2–3):
Abstract local-to-global theorems show that:

Local curvature + local Poincaré/LSI + Lyapunov drift 
⇒
⇒ global Poincaré and LSI, with constants depending only on those local/Drift data.

Yang–Mills structural translation (this part):
For lattice Yang–Mills, the abstract analytic hypotheses become the two concrete conditions:

(YM-Local-FI): local Poincaré and LSI on a bounded, gauge-invariant small-field region 
UΛ⊂ΩΛ

(YM-Lyapunov): existence of volume-uniform Lyapunov functions

a global LSI with constant

hence volume-uniform spectral gaps and strong mixing for the configuration-space dynamics. 

These are exactly the analytic inputs needed in later parts to connect to:

Spectral gaps of OS Hamiltonians via reflection positivity and OS reconstruction, and

Continuum mass gaps via suitable scaling limits.

The hard analytic work that remains is to actually prove (or at least provide convincing bounds for) the two Yang–Mills-specific hypotheses:

Local functional inequalities on a canonical small-field region with volume-uniform constants,

Volume-uniform Lyapunov drift estimates for interacting non-abelian gauge fields.

The next part (Part 15) will turn this structural picture into a precise checklist of analytic tasks for the Yang–Mills mass-gap program, distinguishing clearly between what is already proved in this project and what is still conjectural.

Part 15 — Analytic Checklist for the Yang–Mills Mass-Gap Program

This part collects, in one place, the analytic requirements and consequences of the curvature–diffusion framework developed earlier, with an eye toward the Yang–Mills mass-gap problem. It reorganizes and sharpens the “analytic checklist” originally formulated in the global functional inequality module. 

The goals are:

to separate clearly what is already proved within this framework,

to state precisely what remains as analytic tasks for lattice Yang–Mills,

and to indicate how these pieces interact with spectral gaps and mass gaps downstream.

Throughout, we stay at finite lattice spacing and finite volume; all limits are relegated to later parts.

The analytic checklist of this part indicates where the configuration-side information can feed into the OS-side story:

Making this bridge fully rigorous requires additional work (comparison of generators, OS reconstruction in gauge-invariant sectors, control of scaling limits), which is addressed conceptually in the later OS/continuum parts.

16.4.3.1. Why Wilson’s action is reflection positive

One does not want to reprove the entire reflection positivity of Wilson gauge theory here; it’s a well-known and nontrivial result. But the structural reason is:

The Wilson action can be decomposed as a sum of terms that either live entirely in positive time, entirely in negative time, or straddle the reflection plane in a way that can be written as a sum of squares;

The product Haar measure is invariant under time reflection 
θ

When you integrate

18.4. Structural constraints from configuration-space control

Although a full spectral-to-mass comparison is not currently proven, the existing geometric–analytic framework does impose structural constraints on what can happen to

18.4.1. Heuristic semigroup domination

Imagine that we have, uniformly in

A configuration-space spectral gap

In particular, one does not expect arbitrarily low-energy, highly extended excitations built from smooth, local gauge-invariant functionals, unless they are associated to genuine long-range phenomena (e.g. spontaneous symmetry breaking, which is not expected here).

Heuristically, this suggests that if

18.4.2. Volume-uniform functional inequalities as a necessary ingredient

Regardless of whether Conjecture 
DΛ
D
Λ
	​

 turns out to be true, the framework developed so far strongly indicates that any approach to a uniform OS mass gap must secure volume-uniform configuration-space control:

Uniform Poincaré inequality for gauge-invariant observables,

Uniform Lyapunov drift driving the dynamics towards a small-field region,

Uniform log-Sobolev (or super-Poincaré) inequality in the gauge-invariant sector, at least in a neighborhood of the vacuum.

These are exactly the properties that arise from the local horizontal curvature bound of Part II combined with the local-to-global FI machinery of Part III, under the analytic hypotheses YM-Local-FI and YM-Lyapunov (Parts 14–15). In that sense, the curvature–controlled Gibbs machinery is not just sufficient, but very plausibly necessary, for a clean analytic proof of a nontrivial OS mass gap.

Part 19 — Continuum and Infinite-Volume Limits: Scenarios for a Yang–Mills Mass Gap

In Parts I–III we worked entirely at the level of finite-dimensional configuration manifolds and finite-volume Gibbs measures. In Parts IV–V we put this into the Osterwalder–Schrader (OS) framework and asked what it would take for these finite theories to converge to a continuum Yang–Mills theory with a mass gap. 

This part collects the continuum/infinite-volume side of the story and organizes it into a few fairly concrete scenarios. Nothing in this section is a theorem about continuum Yang–Mills; it is a catalogue of what the finite-volume / curvature / OS machinery would imply if the right uniform estimates hold.

Roughly:

So, in slogan form:

The continuum mass-gap scenarios must respect both sets of constraints.

Exactly how restrictive this is remains to be worked out; Part 18 sketched some structural possibilities, but we do not yet have a theorem of the form “uniform configuration-space LSI + OS axioms ⇒ lower bound on continuum mass”.

Part 20 — Summary and Status of the Continuum Mass-Gap Problem

Parts 1–19 constructed a finite-dimensional, finite-volume, OS-compatible framework for lattice Yang–Mills and identified the geometric and analytic structures that would, in principle, support a uniform finite-volume mass gap:

Curvature and Hessians on configuration space (Bakry–Émery, Haar mass, Wilson Hessian),

Local-to-global functional inequalities (Poincaré, LSI) via curvature and Lyapunov drift,

OS reconstruction and finite-volume Hamiltonians,

Structural comparisons between configuration-space gaps and OS gaps,

Scenarios for continuum and infinite-volume limits.

This final part summarizes:

The hypothetical full chain from lattice curvature to a continuum Yang–Mills mass gap.

How this chain lines up with the Clay mass-gap formulation.

The status of the main analytic and constructive tasks.

The conceptual lessons and limitations of the curvature/FI approach.

Some future directions suggested by the framework.

20.2. Alignment with the Clay Yang–Mills mass-gap formulation

The Clay Yang–Mills problem (in the 
SU(N)
SU(N) case) may be summarized as the request to:

Construct a mathematically rigorous, nontrivial quantum Yang–Mills theory on 
R4
R
4
,

Show that the corresponding Hamiltonian 
H
H has a spectral gap

Δ:=inf⁡(σ(H)∖{0})>0,
Δ:=inf(σ(H)∖{0})>0,

Verify the Osterwalder–Schrader axioms, local gauge invariance, and standard field-theoretic properties (existence of Euclidean and Minkowski measures, locality, etc.).

Within the present framework:

The lattice theory provides a nonperturbative regularization at finite cutoff.

Reflection positivity and OS reconstruction give finite-volume Hamiltonians 
HΛ
H
Λ
	​

 with good positivity and gauge structure.

Bakry–Émery curvature, functional inequalities, and Lyapunov techniques offer quantitative control over the Euclidean finite-volume measures 
μΛ
μ
Λ
	​

 (and hence over fluctuations and correlation lengths).

The remaining Clay-relevant steps, in this language, are to:

Pass to a continuum OS measure on gauge-equivalence classes of connections,

Construct a limiting Hamiltonian 
H
H via OS reconstruction in the continuum,

Show that the spectral gap 
Δ>0
Δ>0 persists in this limit.

The geometric/analytic apparatus developed here does not, by itself, solve these tasks, but it proposes a concrete way to control spectral gaps along the entire lattice-to-continuum trajectory, provided the missing analytic and constructive pieces can be supplied.

20.3.3. Continuum and infinite-volume limit

Open (constructive):

Existence of a continuum Yang–Mills measure.
Prove that lattice Yang–Mills measures (or their space–time versions) converge, along appropriate scaling trajectories, to a continuum Euclidean gauge-field measure 
μ
μ on 
R4
R
4
 (modulo gauge), satisfying OS axioms. This entails tightness, identification of subsequential limits, and handling gauge symmetry in infinite dimension.

OS axioms in the continuum.
Show that continuum Schwinger functions satisfy reflection positivity, Euclidean invariance, clustering, and locality. These properties are delicate in four dimensions and form a major part of constructive quantum field theory.

Control of spectral gaps along the scaling trajectory.
Demonstrate that finite-volume mass gaps 
ΔΛ(a)
Δ
Λ
	​

(a) stay bounded away from zero (or at least do not collapse too quickly) as 
a→0
a→0 and 
∣Λ∣→∞
∣Λ∣→∞, for an appropriate choice of bare coupling 
g(a)
g(a). This is where volume- and cutoff-uniform functional inequalities are expected to be useful, but a full scaling analysis is not yet available.

Identification of the continuum Hamiltonian and mass gap.
Given a continuum OS measure, construct the continuum Hamiltonian 
H
H via OS reconstruction and prove

inf⁡(σ(H)∖{0})>0.
inf(σ(H)∖{0})>0.

This is the Clay mass gap, and remains far beyond current techniques in 4D nonabelian Yang–Mills.

These continuum and infinite-volume steps are not addressed in full generality here; they are highlighted as necessary extensions of the finite-dimensional framework, not as claims of completeness.

20.4. Conceptual lessons and limitations of the curvature/FI approach

The curvature–functional-inequality perspective developed in this project allows a few fairly clean conceptual takeaways.

Geometric sources of mass scales.
The combination of:

Haar geometry on 
G
G (positive Ricci curvature),

The Wilson action’s Hessian in physical directions,

The discrete cochain/Hodge structure,

produces a local mass scale in the finite-lattice theory, visible simultaneously in the Bakry–Émery curvature tensor and in the small-field Hessian of the action. This is a purely geometric origin of a mass parameter at the lattice level.

Functional inequalities as “mass proxies”.
Uniform Poincaré and log-Sobolev inequalities in finite volume behave like mass proxies:

Poincaré inequalities control variances and give uniform spectral gaps for configuration-space dynamics.

LSIs give hypercontractivity and strong concentration, constraining the growth of correlation lengths.

These are not mass gaps in the physical Hamiltonian sense, but they are robust analytic signals that suppress long-range fluctuations in configuration space.

Intrinsic limitations without spectral-to-mass comparison.
Without a result of the 
DΛ
D
Λ
	​

-type, a configuration-space spectral gap cannot be directly translated into an OS Hamiltonian mass gap, even at fixed lattice spacing:

The diffusion semigroup 
PtΛ
P
t
Λ
	​

 and the OS semigroup 
e−tHΛ
e
−tH
Λ
	​

 live on different Hilbert spaces, built from the same measure but with different inner products and time directions.

The transfer matrix depends in a subtle way on the gauge-orbit geometry and the action, beyond mere curvature.

New ideas are needed to connect these objects at a quantitative level.

Configuration-time vs Euclidean-time.
The spectral gap of 
−LΛinv
−L
Λ
inv
	​

 rules out slow relaxation in configuration-time, but does not automatically preclude very long correlation lengths in Euclidean time. Configuration-time control is a strong analytic handle, yet it is not a substitute for a full OS/continuum construction.

In short, curvature and FI give a powerful set of tools for understanding where mass scales live in the lattice theory and how they can be stabilized under changes of volume; they do not automatically provide the final bridge to continuum mass gaps.

20.5. Overall status and future directions

From the viewpoint of the Yang–Mills mass-gap problem, the present framework delivers:

A clear geometric and analytic architecture for studying mass scales in lattice YM:

configuration manifolds with explicit curvature,

small-field regions with controlled Hessians,

Lyapunov/curvature mechanisms for global FI.

A precise formulation of the finite-volume analytic tasks (YM-Local-FI, YM-Lyapunov, Conjecture 
DΛ
D
Λ
	​

) that would be needed to propagate small-field curvature information all the way to finite-volume OS Hamiltonian gaps.

A conceptual bridge between nonperturbative geometry (Haar + Wilson curvature, discrete Hodge structure) and probabilistic/functional tools (Bakry–Émery calculus, Poincaré/LSI, Lyapunov functions).

However, at its current stage, the framework does not:

Construct a continuum 4D Yang–Mills theory,

Prove a nonzero continuum mass gap 
Δ>0
Δ>0,

Settle the key spectral-comparison conjectures 
DΛ
D
Λ
	​

 or their continuum analogues.

Natural future directions include:

Explicit volume-uniform Lyapunov functionals.
Develop concrete 
WΛ
W
Λ
	​

 tailored to interacting lattice gauge fields, with proofs of uniform drift. This may require combining curvature estimates, large-deviation heuristics, and careful control of nonperturbative tails.

Dirichlet-form and semigroup comparisons.
Explore rigorous comparisons between configuration-space diffusions and OS semigroups, possibly via:

Holley–Stroock-type comparison theorems,

coupling or intertwining constructions,

operator inequalities relating their generators.

Testing the curvature/FI program in simpler models.
Apply similar curvature/Lyapunov techniques to more tractable settings:

lower-dimensional Yang–Mills or principal chiral models,

abelian or partially abelian theories,

dimensionally reduced or supersymmetric variants with known continuum limits.

Interfacing with renormalization group analysis.
Investigate whether curvature-based mass scales and FI constants can be tracked along RG flows, and whether dichotomy theorems (massive vs massless phases) can be formulated in BE/FI language.

In summary, the 20-part construction develops a geometrically grounded, analytically sharp finite-volume framework for Yang–Mills mass scales and clarifies exactly which finite-dimensional and continuum steps remain to be solved. Closing those gaps would require major advances at the intersection of Riemannian geometry, functional inequalities, constructive QFT, and lattice gauge theory—but the path and the bottlenecks are now explicitly mapped.
